\displaystyle G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}=\frac{(d-2)}{2r^{\prime}r^{d-2}}\left[r^{d-3}\left(\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{2}-1\right)\right]^{\prime}\,\,.

(124)

14.2

C^{0}
metrics

For the purpose of the thesis, we assume
metrics with the form of Eq. (
118
) to be smooth
except when there is the presence of a spherical matter thin shell,
described by the hypersurface
\mathcal{C}
. In such case, the
hypersurface
\mathcal{C}
separates the space
M
into
the inner region
M_{1}
and the outer region
M_{2}
with metrics

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{1}=b_{1}(u)^{2}\frac{b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})^{2}}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})^{2}}d\tau^{2}+a_{1}(u)^{2}du^{2}+r(u)^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(125)

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{2}=b_{2}(u)^{2}d\tau^{2}+a_{2}(u)^{2}du^{2}+r(u)^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(126)

respectively, where
u_{\mathrm{m}}
is the position label of the
matter thin shell with the hypersurface
\mathcal{C}
being
described by
u=u_{\mathrm{m}}
, and,
b_{1}(u)
,
b_{2}(u)
,

a_{1}(u)
and
a_{2}(u)
are smooth arbitrary functions. The coordinate

u
in
M_{1}
has the range
u\in]0,u_{\mathrm{m}}[
and in
M_{2}
it has
the range
u\in]u_{\mathrm{m}},u[
. The metrics in Eqs. (
125
)
and (
126
) can be described by the metric in Eq. (
118
),
with metric components

\displaystyle b(u)=\begin{cases}\frac{b_{1}(u)b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}&0<u<u_{\mathrm{m}}\\
b_{2}(u)&u_{\mathrm{m}}\leq u<1\end{cases}\,\,,

\displaystyle a(u)=\begin{cases}a_{1}(u)&0<u<u_{\mathrm{m}}\\
a_{2}(u)&u_{\mathrm{m}}\leq u<1\end{cases}\,\,,

(127)

While in this case
b(u)
is continuous,
a(u)
is not necessarily.
Using further a coordinate transformation to a geodesic coordinate

\rho=\int_{u_{\mathrm{m}}}^{u}a(s)ds
,
one turns the discontinuity in
a
into the property that
r(\rho)

is
C^{0}
in function of the geodesic coordinate. The metric
with these properties is then
C^{0}
with the form

\displaystyle ds^{2}=b(\rho)^{2}d\tau^{2}+d\rho^{2}+r(\rho)^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(128)

where

\displaystyle b(\rho)=b_{1}(u(\rho))\frac{b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}\theta[-\rho]+b_{2}(u(\rho))\theta[\rho]\,\,,

(129)

where
\theta[\rho]
is the Heaviside function and the inverse
of
\rho(u)
, i.e.
u(\rho)
was used.

The matter shell and the boundary of the space are described by the hypersurfaces
of constant
u
, i.e.
u=u_{\mathrm{m}}
and
u=1
respectively.
As the previous case of smooth metrics, it
is useful to analyze hypersurfaces of constant
u
,
which have an induced metric as Eq. (
119
),
or explicitly at the shell one has

\displaystyle ds^{2}|_{u=u_{\mathrm{m}}}=b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})^{2}d\tau^{2}+r(u_{\mathrm{m}})^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(130)

and at the boundary one has

\displaystyle ds^{2}|_{u\rightarrow 1}=b_{2}(u\rightarrow 1)^{2}d\tau^{2}+r(u\rightarrow 1)^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(131)

The extrinsic curvature
K_{ab}
of the constant
u
hypersurfaces
is given by Eq. (
120
), taking into account the
metrics in Eqs. (
125
) and (
126
).
Explicitly at the shell, the extrinsic curvature suffers
a jump in general, i.e.
the extrinsic curvature
computed in one side of the shell is not the same as the
one computed at the other side. The jump is defined by
square brackets on a tensor living in the hypersurface,
e.g.
[K_{ab}]=K_{2ab}-K_{1ab}
,
where
K_{2ab}
is the extrinsic curvature evaluated
at the side towards
u>u_{\mathrm{m}}
and

K_{1ab}
is the extrinsic curvature evaluated
at the side towards
u<u_{\mathrm{m}}
. Namely, the extrinsic
curvature at the shell from the side of
M_{1}
is

\displaystyle K_{1ab}dx^{a}dx^{b}=\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}b_{2}^{2}}{a_{1}b_{1}}d\tau^{2}+\frac{r^{\prime}r}{a_{1}}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(132)

and from the side of
M_{2}
is

\displaystyle K_{2ab}dx^{a}dx^{b}=\frac{b^{\prime}_{2}b_{2}}{a_{2}}d\tau^{2}+\frac{r^{\prime}r}{a_{2}}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(133)

where the components are written here in terms of the
coordinate
u
and the prime means the derivative in
u
.
Moreover, the trace of the extrinsic curvature
at each side is given by

\displaystyle K_{1}=\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}}{a_{1}b_{1}}+(d-2)\frac{r^{\prime}}{a_{1}r}\,\,,

\displaystyle K_{2}=\frac{b^{\prime}_{2}}{a_{2}b_{2}}+(d-2)\frac{r^{\prime}}{a_{2}r}\,\,.

(134)

At the boundary of space
u=1
, one has the extrinsic curvature

K_{2ab}
and its trace
K_{2}
with the same form of
Eqs. (
133
) and (
14.2
)
evaluated at
u=1
.

In the presence of a matter thin shell, the
C^{0}
metric
induces Dirac delta terms in the Ricci tensor. We are interested here
on the Ricci scalar and the Einstein tensor in particular,
which in this case have the expression

\displaystyle R=R_{1}\theta[-\rho]+R_{2}\theta[\rho]-2[K]\delta[\rho]\,\,,

\displaystyle G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}={G_{1}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}\theta[-\rho]+{G_{2}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}\theta[\rho]+([K]-[K^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}])\delta[\rho]\,\,,

(135)

where
R_{1}
is the Ricci scalar evaluated at
\rho<0
or
u<u_{\mathrm{m}}
,
R_{2}
is the Ricci
scalar evaluated at
\rho>0
or
u>u_{\mathrm{m}}
,
{G_{1}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}
is the Einstein
tensor component evaluated at
\rho<0
or
u<u_{\mathrm{m}}
, and

{G_{2}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}
is the Einstein
tensor component evaluated at
\rho>0
or
u>u_{\mathrm{m}}
, with these
quantities being given by

\displaystyle R_{1}=-\frac{2}{a_{1}b_{1}r^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}r^{d-2}}{a_{1}}\right)^{\prime}\frac{b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})^{2}}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})^{2}}-2{G_{1}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}\,\,,

(136)

\displaystyle R_{2}=-\frac{2}{a_{2}b_{2}r^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{2}r^{d-2}}{a_{2}}\right)^{\prime}-2{G_{2}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}\,\,,

(137)

\displaystyle{G_{1}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}=\frac{(d-2)}{2r^{\prime}r^{d-2}}\left[r^{d-3}\left(\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a_{1}}\right)^{2}-1\right)\right]^{\prime}\,\,,

(138)

\displaystyle{G_{2}}^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}=\frac{(d-2)}{2r^{\prime}r^{d-2}}\left[r^{d-3}\left(\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a_{2}}\right)^{2}-1\right)\right]^{\prime}\,\,,

(139)

written in terms of the coordinate
u
.
Notice that the expansion in
Eq. (
14.2
) can be computed by writing
the Ricci scalar and the Einstein tensor in terms of the
first and second derivatives of
b
and
r
, use the chain rule

\frac{d}{d\rho}=\frac{1}{a(u)}\frac{d}{du}
, and then use
the expansion in Heaviside functions, with the
identity
\frac{d\theta[\rho]}{d\rho}=\delta(\rho)
.
This is indeed the same procedure as the thin shell
formalism
[
77
]
, where the continuity of the metric
is imposed as the first junction condition.
The expression for the Dirac delta term in the Einstein tensor is given by

\displaystyle[K]-[K^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}]=\frac{(d-2)}{r}\evaluated{\left(\frac{r'}{a_2}
- \frac{r'}{a_1}\right)}_{u=u_\mathrm{m}}\,\,,

(140)

written in terms of the coordinate
u
.
The expression in Eq. (
140
)
is useful further on and constitutes one component of the
gravitational part of the second junction condition.

We have to insert the metric, in Eq. (
118
) for smooth metrics or
in Eqs. (
125
) and (
126
) for
C^{0}
metrics,
in the path integral in Eq. (
116
), together with the
boundary conditions at the hypersurface
u=1

describing the boundary of space
\partial M
and also
the heat reservoir. These boundary conditions are fixed
while performing the path integral. One then should sum over
all the possible metrics on the path integral.
In principle, the sum over the metrics can be decomposed
in terms of their topology class. In the case here treated, the
topology class depends on a set of regularity conditions for the
spherically symmetric metric at
u=0
.
Below, we present the regularity and
boundary conditions used in the thesis for spherically symmetric
metrics.

15
Metric regularity conditions

15.1
Black hole-like conditions

For spherically symmetric metrics, we need to impose regularity conditions
at the center of the space or at its minimal
surface, which for the metric in Eq. (
118
) is
situated at
u=0
. The black hole-like conditions correspond to
the choice

\displaystyle b(0)=0\,\,,\,\,r(0)=r_{+}\,\,,

(141)

for the components of the metric, where
r_{+}
is the
horizon radius.

This choice alone can induce possible
divergences in the Ricci scalar and topological defects,
which here they must be avoided by imposing conditions to the
derivatives of the components of the metric. These conditions can
be found by expanding the metric near
u=0
as

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\left[\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{2}\biggr|_{u=0}\varepsilon^{2}+\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a^{2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{\prime}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}\varepsilon^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4})\right]d\tau^{2}

\displaystyle+d\varepsilon^{2}+\left[r_{+}+(r^{\prime}a^{-1})\bigr|_{u=0}\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\right]^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(142)

where
\epsilon=\int^{\delta}_{0}adu
for small
\delta
, assuming
that
\int^{\delta}_{0}adu
is finite. Otherwise,

u=0
should be understood as a boundary of the space.
The condition
b(0)=0
means that a hypersurface with constant

u
, having topology
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
,
becomes topologically
\{u=0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
in the limit
of
u=0
, i.e.
a point times a
(d-2)
–sphere and the hypersurface volume
becomes zero. This behaviour is precisely described
by the metric in Eq. (
15.1
), namely,
the
(\tau,\varepsilon)
sector describes approximately
the metric of a cone in general, with a possible conical singularity which
introduces a topological defect in the Riemannian space.
In order to avoid the existence of such singularity, we impose the regularity
condition

\displaystyle\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\biggr|_{u=0}=1\,\,,

(143)

and so the
(\tau,\varepsilon)
sector of metric
describes Euclidean flat space near
u=0
. The remaining conditions are
found from avoiding the divergence of the Ricci scalar. The Ricci scalar
near
u=0
is given by

\displaystyle R=-\frac{2(d-2)}{\varepsilon r_{+}}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\Bigr|_{u=0}-\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{a}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{\prime}\right)\Bigr|_{u=0}+\mathcal{O}(1)\,\,,

(144)

and so the regularity conditions are

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\Bigr|_{u=0}=0\,\,,\,\,\left(\frac{1}{a}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{\prime}\right)\Bigr|_{u=0}=0\,\,.

(145)

It is interesting to note that the first equation of Eq. (
145
)
is equivalent, in even dimensions, to the condition that the
Riemannian space must have an Euler characteristic
\chi=2
.

The conditions in Eqs. (
141
)–(
145
) are precisely
the conditions of the metric that one would obtain if the Wick transformation of
a stationary black hole spacetime metric was performed. The
(d-2)
–surface at
u=0
coincides
with the bifurcate
(d-2)
–sphere of the horizon of the stationary black hole. The topology of the
Riemannian space is
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
with these conditions.

15.2
Flat conditions

Other possible regularity conditions are the flat
conditions, which are achieved by choosing

\displaystyle b(0)\,\,\mathrm{finite}\,\mathrm{and}\,\mathrm{non}\,\mathrm{zero}\,\,,\,\,r(0)=0\,\,,

(146)

for the components of the metric. By expanding the
metric near
u=0
with the conditions in Eq. (
146
),
one has

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\left(b(0)+\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)\Bigr|_{u=0}\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\right)d\tau^{2}+d\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\Bigr|_{u=0}\varepsilon+\frac{1}{a}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\right]^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(147)

where again
\varepsilon=\int_{0}^{\delta}adu
for small
\delta
and
it is assumed that
\int_{0}^{\delta}adu
is finite. The remaining
regularity conditions must be extracted from the condition that
the Ricci scalar is well-behaved at
u=0
. The Ricci scalar is

\displaystyle R=-\frac{2(d-2)}{b\varepsilon}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}-\frac{2(d-2)}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{\prime}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle-\frac{(d-2)(d-3)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[1-\left(\frac{a}{r^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\right]\biggr|_{u=0}\,\,,

(148)

near
u=0
. Therefore, in order to avoid the divergence of the Ricci scalar,
the following regularity conditions

\displaystyle\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}=0\,\,,\,\,\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}=1\,\,,\,\,\left(\frac{1}{a}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{\prime}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}=0\,\,,

(149)

are necessary.

The regularity conditions in Eqs. (
146
) and (
149
)
are the conditions of the Riemannian metric if the Wick transformation was performed
to a flat Lorentzian metric. The topology of the Riemannian space with these regularity
conditions is
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.

16
Metric boundary conditions

16.1
Finite cavity

The boundary conditions that we impose at the boundary of the
Riemannian space for the metric are given here
by the Dirichlet boundary conditions. In the case of
the spherically symmetric metric in Eq. (
118
),
the boundary of the space is positioned at
u=1
with
induced metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}\bigr|_{u=1}=b(1)^{2}d\tau^{2}+r(1)^{2}d\Omega_{d-2}^{2}\,\,.

(150)

According to the Dirichlet boundary conditions, for
a finite boundary, we must fix

\displaystyle\beta=2\pi b(1)\,\,,\,\,R=r(1)\,\,,

(151)

that is, we must fix the inverse temperature
\beta
of the
spherical shell at
u=1
, representing the heat reservoir,
that is given as the total imaginary time length, and moreover
we fix the radius
R
of the shell. We therefore
have a Riemannian space which represents a
finite cavity, assuming the regularity conditions in the previous
section.

16.2
Infinite cavity: zero cosmological constant

For the case where the boundary of the space is infinite, i.e.
when
r(u)\bigr|_{u\rightarrow 1}
is infinite,
the boundary conditions of the Riemannian space are
given according to the asymptotic behaviour of the metric when

u\rightarrow 1
.
The cases for zero and negative cosmological constant must be
analyzed separately as the metric has different
asymptotic behaviour.

When the cosmological constant is zero, the boundary conditions
imposed are the same as the asymptotically flat spacetime
conditions but translated to Riemannian space. In this sense,
the behaviour of the metric components must be that

\displaystyle b(u)\bigr|_{u\rightarrow 1}=\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\,\,,\,\,\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\Bigr|_{u\rightarrow 1}=1\,\,,

(152)

where
b(u)\bigr|_{u\rightarrow 1}
must be a fixed constant and
it is given by
\beta
, the inverse temperature measured at infinity.

16.3
Infinite cavity: negative cosmological constant

When the cosmological constant is negative, for an infinite
hypersurface
\partial M
, the boundary conditions
imposed are the ones of asymptotically anti-de Sitter or AdS, but translated
to Riemannian space. This amounts to the metric satisfying asymptotically the
Euclidean Einstein equations with a negative cosmological
constant, i.e.
R_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{(d-1)}{l^{2}}g_{\alpha\beta}

and fixing the remaining freedom in the metric. In order to
put these conditions in terms of the components of the metric and
their asymptotic behaviours, we must perform a conformal transformation
in the metric
g_{\alpha\beta}=w^{2}\bar{g}_{\alpha\beta}
, where

\bar{g}_{\alpha\beta}
is the conformal metric and
w
is the conformal
factor. In order to have a nonsingular conformal metric, the
conformal transformation must have a behaviour
w=\frac{c}{r(u)}
, where

c
is a constant. We choose
c=1
. We further make the coordinate
transformation
w=w(u)
so that the conformal metric assumes
the form in the neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}(\partial M)

of the hypersurface
\partial M
as

\displaystyle d\bar{s}^{2}\bigr|_{\mathcal{N}(\partial M)}=\frac{b(u)^{2}}{r(u)^{2}}d\tau^{2}+\left(\frac{a(u)r(u)}{r^{\prime}(u)}\right)^{2}dw(u)^{2}+d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,.

(153)

The hypersurface
\partial M
is then defined by
w=0
.
The asymptotic behaviour of the metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
translates into
conditions for the metric
\bar{g}_{\alpha\beta}
,
see
[
162
,
163
]
. In fact, the condition

R_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{(d-1)}{l^{2}}g_{\alpha\beta}
can be
split into two conditions for the conformal metric, namely that the
boundary
w=0
is described by the metric
d\bar{s}^{2}|_{w=0}=d\bar{\tau}^{2}+d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}
and that
\bar{g}^{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\alpha}w\nabla_{\beta}w=\frac{1}{l^{2}}
, where
\bar{\tau}
is proportional to
\tau
by some constant.
And so the boundary conditions chosen for the metric elements are

\displaystyle\frac{b(u)}{r(u)}\Bigr|_{u\rightarrow 1}=\frac{\bar{\beta}}{2\pi l}\,\,,\,\,\frac{a(u)r(u)}{r^{\prime}(u)}\Bigr|_{u\rightarrow 1}=l\,\,,

(154)

where
\bar{\beta}
is defined as the inverse temperature measured at the
conformal boundary of the asymptotically AdS
space.

17
The gravitational path integral in spherical
symmetry

17.1
Smooth metrics

17.1.1
General considerations

We now proceed to reduce the path integral in Eq. (
116
)
to the case of spherically symmetric Riemannian spaces. For the
case of zero cosmological constant, one has the partition function

Z=\int Dg_{\alpha\beta}D\psi\mathrm{e}^{-I_{g}[g_{\mu\nu}]-I_{m}[g_{\mu\nu},\psi]}
with the gravitational action being
given by

\displaystyle I_{g}=-\frac{1}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}(R-2\Lambda)\sqrt{g}d^{d}x-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}K\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x-I_{\mathrm{ref}}\,\,.

(155)

For the case of a smooth metric, one can use the expression of the
Ricci scalar in Eq. (
123
) and the expression of
the trace of the extrinsic curvature in
Eq. (
121
) to obtain

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}R\sqrt{g}d^{d}x=-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}r^{d-2}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}+\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}r^{d-2}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}abr^{d-2}G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x\,\,,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}K\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x=-\frac{2\pi}{\mu}\left(\frac{br^{\prime}r^{d-3}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}r^{d-2}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}\,\,,

(156)

where
\sqrt{g}=abr^{d-2}
,
\sqrt{\gamma}=br^{d-2}
and

\mu=\frac{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}{(d-2)\Omega_{d-2}}
.
Putting together the expressions in Eqs. (
17.1.1
)
into Eq. (
155
),
one finally has

\displaystyle I_{g}=-\left(\frac{2\pi br^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}r^{d-2}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}abr^{d-2}(G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}+\Lambda)d^{d}x-I_{\mathrm{ref}}\,\,.

(157)

The form of the gravitational action in Eq. (
157
)
assumes the form of the typical decomposition of the space in a foliation
of hypersurfaces, with
G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}
term and the boundary term at

u\rightarrow 1
being part of the Hamiltonian of the space.
The remaining terms are determined by the regularity and
boundary conditions, and also by the choice of the reference space. It is interesting
to note that the term at
u=0
seems to be topological, due to its link with the
regularity conditions.

17.1.2
Zero cosmological constant

For the case of zero cosmological constant, the reference space is
the Riemannian space obtained by performing the map to flat Lorentzian
spacetime at the same temperature, which we call hot flat space, giving

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\mathrm{flat}}=b(1)^{2}d\tau^{2}+dr^{2}+r^{2}d\Omega_{d-2}^{2}\,\,,

(158)

where the coordinate transformation
r=r(y)
was performed and

r\in\,]0,r(1)[
. It is important to distinguish this space from the
flat Riemannian space since the former has topology
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
while the latter has topology
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The
action for hot flat space can be written as

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{flat}}=-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}K_{\mathrm{flat}}d^{d-1}x\,\,,

(159)

or alternatively can be obtained from Eq. (
157
)
by setting
\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)_{\mathrm{flat}}=1
,

\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)_{\mathrm{flat}}=0
,

G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}=0
, with
\Lambda=0
and flat regular conditions,
yielding

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{flat}}=-\frac{2\pi}{\mu}\left(br^{d-3}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}\,\,.

(160)

The gravitational action for a spherically smooth metric with zero cosmological
constant is then

\displaystyle I_{g\mathrm{f}}=\left(\frac{2\pi br^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(1-\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}r^{d-2}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}abr^{d-2}G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x\,\,.

(161)

17.1.3
Negative cosmological constant

For negative cosmological constant, the reference space chosen is the
Riemannian space obtained from performing the map to AdS
spacetime, which we call hot AdS space, at the same temperature.
The metric describing hot AdS space is

\displaystyle ds_{\mathrm{AdS}}^{2}=b_{\mathrm{AdS}}(y(r))^{2}d\tau^{2}+\left(\frac{a}{r^{\prime}}(r)\right)^{2}_{\mathrm{AdS}}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(162)

where
b_{\mathrm{AdS}}(1)=b(1)
,

\left(\frac{b^{\prime}}{a}\right)_{\mathrm{AdS}}\Bigr|_{u=0}=0

and
\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)_{\mathrm{AdS}}^{2}=1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}
.
The action for the hot AdS space is more contrived compared to hot flat space,
since the Ricci scalar is
R=-\frac{d(d-1)}{l^{2}}
, and it is
given by

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{AdS}}=\frac{(d-1)}{8\pi l^{2}l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}\sqrt{g}d^{d}x-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}K_{\mathrm{AdS}}\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x\,\,.

(163)

Alternatively, one can use also Eq. (
157
) to evaluate
the action of hot AdS space by using the expression of the components of the
metric at the boundary, plus that
G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}=-\Lambda
and
the flat regularity conditions, to obtain

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{AdS}}=-\left(\frac{2\pi br^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)_{\mathrm{AdS}}\right)_{u\rightarrow 1}\,\,.

(164)

The gravitational action for a smooth spherically symmetric metric
with a negative cosmological constant is then

\displaystyle I_{gl}=\left(\frac{2\pi br^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)_{\mathrm{AdS}}-\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}r^{d-2}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}abr^{d-2}\left(G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}-\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2l^{2}})\right)d^{d}x\,\,.

(165)

17.2

C^{0}
metrics

17.2.1
General considerations

For the case where the metric is
C^{0}
with non-differentiability
at the hypersurface
\mathcal{C}
, described by
u=u_{\mathrm{m}}
, the gravitational
action can be given by

\displaystyle I_{g}=-\frac{1}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{1}}(R_{1}-2\Lambda)\sqrt{g}d^{d}x-\frac{1}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{2}}(R_{2}-2\Lambda)\sqrt{g}d^{d}x

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}}[K]\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}K\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x-I_{\mathrm{ref}}\,\,,

(166)

where Eq. (
14.2
) was used to decompose the Ricci
scalar in terms of Heaviside functions and the Dirac delta. Another
way of getting Eq. (
17.2.1
) is by
summing the Einstein-Hilbert action with the Gibbons-Hawking-York
boundary term in each region
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, noting that
the difference on the trace of the extrinsic curvature is not zero.
The integrals can be decomposed in terms of
the spherically symmetric metric components as

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{1}}R\sqrt{g}d^{d}x=-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})r^{d-2}}{a_{1}b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})r^{d-2}}{a_{1}b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}\right)\biggr|_{u=u_{\mathrm{m}}}+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{1}}a_{1}b_{1}\frac{b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}r^{d-2}G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x\,\,,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{2}}R_{2}\sqrt{g}d^{d}x=-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{2}r^{d-2}}{a_{2}}\right)\biggr|_{u=u_{\mathrm{m}}}+\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{2}r^{d-2}}{a_{2}}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{2}}a_{2}b_{2}r^{d-2}G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x\,\,,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}}[K]\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x=-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}}([K^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}]-[K])\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x

\displaystyle+\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{2}r^{d-2}}{a_{2}}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow u_{\mathrm{m}}}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})r^{d-2}}{a_{1}b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow u_{\mathrm{m}}}\,\,,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}K\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x=-\frac{2\pi}{\mu}\left(\frac{b_{2}r^{\prime}r^{d-3}}{a_{2}}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{2}r^{d-2}}{a_{2}}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}\,\,.

(167)

Putting together the terms in Eq. (
17.2.1
), the action for the spherically
symmetric
C^{0}
metric is

\displaystyle I_{g}=-\frac{2\pi}{\mu}\left(\frac{b_{2}r^{\prime}r^{d-3}}{a_{2}}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})r^{d-2}}{a_{1}b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{1}}a_{1}b_{1}\frac{b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}r^{d-2}(G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}+\Lambda)d^{d}x+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{2}}a_{2}b_{2}r^{d-2}(G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}+\Lambda)d^{d}x

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}}([K^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}]-[K])\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x-I_{\mathrm{ref}}\,\,,

(168)

which is basically Eq. (
157
) but with
b(u)
,
a(u)
and

G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}
expanded in Heaviside functions and Dirac delta.
The non-smoothness of the metric leads to the additional boundary term of the action
at the hypersurface
\mathcal{C}
compared to the action of smooth metrics.
Indeed, this is expected as the term
[K^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}]-[K]
represents the
junction condition for the shell that comes from the Dirac delta term of
G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}
.

17.2.2
Zero cosmological constant

The action for spherically symmetric
C^{0}
metric with zero cosmological constant
follows from the analysis with the smooth metric. The action for the reference
space is
I_{\mathrm{flat}}
from Eq. (
159
), which
in terms of the metric components is

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{flat}}=-\frac{2\pi}{\mu}\left(b_{2}r^{d-3}\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}\,\,.

(169)

And so the action of a
C^{0}
metric with a zero cosmological constant in Eq. (
17.2.1
)
becomes

\displaystyle I_{g\mathrm{f}}=\left(\frac{2\pi b_{2}r^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(1-\frac{r^{\prime}}{a_{2}}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})r^{d-2}}{a_{1}b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{1}}a_{1}b_{1}\frac{b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}r^{d-2}G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{2}}a_{2}b_{2}r^{d-2}G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}}([K^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}]-[K])\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x\,\,.

(170)

17.2.3
Negative cosmological constant

For the case of negative cosmological constant, the reference action
is
I_{\mathrm{AdS}}
from Eq. (
163
), which in terms of
the metric components is

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{AdS}}=-\left(\frac{2\pi b_{2}r^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a_{2}}\right)_{\mathrm{AdS}}\right)_{u\rightarrow 1}\,\,.

(171)

Therefore, the action of a spherically symmetric
C^{0}
metric
with negative cosmological constant, in Eq. (
17.2.1
),
becomes

\displaystyle I_{gl}=\left(\frac{2\pi b_{2}r^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)_{\mathrm{AdS}}-\frac{r^{\prime}}{a_{2}}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u\rightarrow 1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}_{1}b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})r^{d-2}}{a_{1}b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{1}}a_{1}b_{1}\frac{b_{2}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}{b_{1}(u_{\mathrm{m}})}r^{d-2}\left(G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}-\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2l^{2}}\right)d^{d}x

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M_{2}}a_{2}b_{2}r^{d-2}\left(G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}-\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2l^{2}}\right)d^{d}x

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}}([K^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}]-[K])\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x\,\,.

(172)

18
The statistical path integral and its connection to thermodynamics

With the shape of the action decomposed into the spherically symmetric
metric components, the Euclidean path integral is composed by the sum
over the possible paths of the metric components and matter fields as

\displaystyle Z=\int DbDaDrD\psi\,\mathrm{e}^{-I_{g}-I_{m}}\,\,,

(173)

where
I_{g}
can be given by
I_{g\mathrm{f}}
in the case of
zero cosmological constant or
I_{gl}
for the case of negative
cosmological constant, with the sum being made over components
obeying the boundary conditions and over the possible
regularity conditions.

As discussed above, even in this form, an expression for the
path integral seems quite elusive. Typically, one performs the
saddle point approximation to find the paths of the metric
components
b
,
a
,
r
and
\psi
, that minimize the
full action. We are going to apply this approximation in the upcoming chapters
for the particular cases of interest, namely for black hole spaces
with a static electromagnetic field or with matter, and also for
the case of a self-gravitating matter thin shell. In the saddle
point approximation, one can consider only the zeroth order
contribution which translates into a partition function

Z=\mathrm{e}^{-I_{0}}
, where
I_{0}
is the action evaluated
at a minimum path. This is the zero loop approximation.

Depending on the ensemble, the partition function is tied
to a thermodynamic potential. This can be seen from the
definition of the partition function and the possible mean
thermodynamic values that one can obtain. For example,
in the canonical ensemble, with the inverse temperature
and area fixed, one can obtain the mean energy through

\displaystyle E=-\frac{\partial\log(Z)}{\partial\beta}\,\,,

(174)

since
-\frac{\partial\log(Z)}{\partial\beta}
can be
formally written as
\sum_{i}E_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta E_{i}}/Z
,
where the
i
subscript means with respect to each microstate.
Moreover, the entropy is defined as the Gibbs entropy with
the formula
S=-\sum_{i}p_{i}\log(p_i)
, with
p_{i}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta E_{i}}/Z
,
which can be written in terms of
\log(Z)
as

\displaystyle S=-\beta\frac{\partial\log(Z)}{\partial\beta}+\log(Z)\,\,.

(175)

Using the formula for the energy and the entropy, the partition
function can be related to the free energy
F

in the canonical ensemble as

\displaystyle\beta F=-\log(Z)\,\,,

(176)

where the free energy is defined by the Legendre transform
of the mean energy,
F=E-TS
. We can now establish the
connection between the zero loop approximation and thermodynamics.
Since
\log(Z)=-I_{0}
, then we have

\displaystyle F=TI_{0}\,\,,

(177)

which means that the action evaluated at the minimizing paths
translates into the free energy of the ensemble. Having the
action in the zero loop approximation, we can obtain straightforwardly
the free energy and the remaining thermodynamic quantities,
i.e. the mean energy, the entropy and the
pressure, by calculating the derivatives of the free energy.

A similar analysis holds for the case of the
grand canonical ensemble, where the thermodynamic
potential that has the connection with the partition
function is the grand potential
W=-\log(Z)
, where
we define
W=E-TS-\mu N
, with
\mu
being
a chemical potential and
N
being a mean number. Hence,
the grand potential is given by the action at the
minimum path as
W=TI_{0}
, and we can obtain the
thermodynamic quantities by calculating the derivatives
of
W
or
I_{0}
.

19
Summary

In this chapter, we gave an introduction to the construction of statistical
ensembles of curved spacetimes, here with focus on the metric.
In order to study the statistical ensemble of a configuration described
by a stationary Lorentzian spacetime, with a timelike hypersurface as
the boundary and describing the heat reservoir, we must perform a
map of the spacetime to a Riemannian or pseudo-Riemannian metric.
The shape of the Riemannian or pseudo-Riemannian
metric has to be relaxed except for the
fixed data at the boundary, which must be the same data of the
configuration that we want to study. For spherically symmetric
spaces, we impose Dirichlet boundary conditions that compose the fixed data
at the boundary space, which in this case is described by a spherical shell
and the inverse temperature of the ensemble is fixed to be
the total imaginary time length at the boundary. The partition function
is then given by the Euclidean path integral over the Riemannian or
pseudo-Riemannian metrics with fixed boundary data. For spherically
symmetric metrics, we also need to sum over the discrete set of
regularity conditions which are tied to the topology of the
Riemannian space.

Here, we restricted the shape of the metric to spherically symmetric metrics as the
boundary data is given for a spherical shell. We explained the possible regularity
and boundary conditions, which are going to be used
in the next chapters. Moreover, we decomposed the gravitational action
in terms of the spherically symmetric metric components, ready to be
used for the analysis of specific configurations including matter.
We performed the calculations in this chapter to avoid repetition
in the following chapters.

Finally, we established the connection of the partition function through the
Euclidean path integral with the thermodynamics of the ensemble.
In order to obtain the partition function, we are going to employ the
zero loop approximation in the upcoming chapters.
The action evaluated at the minimizing paths gives the
relevant thermodynamic potential of the ensemble and through
its derivatives, we can obtain the thermodynamic properties of the
system. This analysis is expanded further in the upcoming chapters.

†
†
margin:

4

Grand canonical ensemble of a
d
-dimensional
Reissner-Nordström black hole in a cavity

20
Introduction

As previously discussed in Chapter
3
,
the Euclidean path integral approach
[
67
]
allows the construction
of statistical ensembles in curved spaces. Moreover, the use of the zero loop
approximation allows the computation of the partition function in terms of the
classical paths of the action. The approach was extended to the York
formalism
[
68
,
115
]
,
where a finite cavity is introduced, allowing for stable equilibrium configurations.
The formalism was used to construct the grand canonical ensemble of a
charged black hole inside a cavity in four dimensions
[
130
,
131
]
and
for black branes
[
143
]
. Also, York’s analysis was extended to higher
dimensions
[
101
,
102
]
, where there was an emphasis on the
connection between the statistical ensemble and matter dynamic stability
in curved spacetime. Namely, the two black hole solutions of the ensemble
bifurcate when the cavity is at the light ring radius, and also the stable
black hole starts to be more favorable than hot flat space when its radius corresponds
to the Buchdahl bound.

Motivated by these developments, in this chapter, we construct the
grand canonical ensemble of a Reissner-Nordström black hole inside a
cavity in higher dimensions using the Euclidean path integral
approach to quantum gravity, with fixed temperature and fixed electric
potential. We perform the zero loop approximation in steps.
First, the Hamiltonian and Gauss constraints are imposed to find a reduced action
and then the stationary points of the reduced action are found, corresponding to
black hole solutions of the ensemble. It is found that there are up to two solutions
of the ensemble, with its qualitative behaviour being presented. The two solutions
bifurcate at a certain ratio between horizon radius and cavity radius,
which does not correspond to the light ring ratio. The main objective was to study
the phase transitions and read off possible connections to matter dynamic stability.
We therefore analyzed the phase transitions
between the black hole solutions and hot flat space, finding a first order phase
transition. We found that
the black hole solution is more favorable when its radius is slightly lower than the
Buchdahl-Andréasson-Wright bound
[
129
]
,
which is related to the maximum compactness of a
charged configuration obeying certain energy conditions. We present in detail the five dimensional case,
d=5
, where an analytic
expression for the black hole solutions was found.

This chapter is organized as follows. In Sec.
21
, we consider the
partition of the grand canonical ensemble for spherically symmetric
metrics, obeying regularity and boundary conditions, namely the fixed inverse temperature
is established as the total imaginary proper time at the boundary of the cavity and the
radius of the cavity is fixed. In Sec.
22
, we perform the zero loop approximation,
where we first impose the constraints to find the reduced action and we find the stationary
points of the reduced action. In Sec.
23
, we obtain the thermodynamics
of the system from the partition function in the zero loop approximation
and we further analyze the possible phase transitions.
In Sec.
24
, we present in detail the five dimensional case,
d=5
.
In Sec.
25
, we compare the bifurcation radius with the light ring radius, and
also the thermodynamic radius with the Buchdahl-Andreásson-Wright bound.
In Sec.
27
, we conclude the chapter. We note that the work in this chapter
is based on
[
2
]
.

21
The grand canonical ensemble of a charged black hole
in the Euclidean path integral approach

21.1
The partition function

Through the Euclidean path integral approach, we can
construct the grand canonical ensemble of a charged black hole
inside a finite cavity, in
d
dimensions, by considering the
partition function

\displaystyle Z=\int Dg_{\alpha\beta}DA_{\gamma}\,\mathrm{e}^{-I[g_{\mu\nu},A_{\sigma}]}\,\,,

(178)

with the Euclidean action

\displaystyle I=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\frac{R}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}-\frac{F_{ab}F^{ab}}{4}\right)\sqrt{g}d^{d}x-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}(K-K_{0})\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x\,,

(179)

where
R
is the Ricci scalar,
g
is the determinant of the Euclidean
metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
,
F_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}A_{\alpha}
is the strength field tensor of the
Maxwell vector potential
A_{\alpha}
,
\gamma
is the determinant of
the induced metric
\gamma_{ab}
of the hypersurface describing the
boundary
\partial M
,
K=n^{\alpha}_{;\alpha}
is the trace of the
extrinsic curvature of the hypersurface with
n^{\alpha}
being the
outward unit normal to it, and
K_{0}
is the
extrinsic curvature of the boundary embedded in flat space, giving
the action of hot flat space. The action in Eq. (
179
)
can be split into
I=I_{g\mathrm{f}}+I_{A}
, where

\displaystyle I_{g\mathrm{f}}=-\frac{1}{16\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}R\sqrt{g}d^{d}x-\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{\partial M}(K-K_{0})\sqrt{\gamma}d^{d-1}x\,,

(180)

\displaystyle I_{A}=\int_{M}\frac{F_{ab}F^{ab}}{4}\sqrt{g}d^{d}x\,\,.

(181)

The path integral in the partition function in Eq. (
178
)
is performed along Riemannian metrics with fixed boundary conditions, which are
periodic in the imaginary time. We make a reminder that among the possible
paths are Riemannian metrics that correspond to physical static Lorentzian
spacetimes by a Wick transformation in the imaginary time. For further details
on the construction of the path integral,
see Chapter
3
.

21.2
Geometry and boundary conditions

In this case, we consider the boundary of space to be described by a spherical shell
with fixed temperature and electric potential. This means we are dealing with
the grand canonical ensemble. Due to the spherical symmetry of the boundary,
the metrics with spherical symmetry should contribute the most
to the path integral. And so, we restrict the path integral
to spherically symmetric metrics of the form

\displaystyle ds^{2}=b(u)^{2}d\tau^{2}+a(u)^{2}du^{2}+r(u)^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,\,,

(182)

where
b(u)
,
a(u)
and
r(u)
are arbitrary smooth functions of
u
,
the coordinates have the range
\tau\in\,]0,2\pi[
and
u\in\,]0,1[
,
and
d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}
is the
(d-2)
–sphere line element.

In principle, the path integral should include a sum over topologies of
the Riemannian space with a metric of the form of Eq. (
182
).
The sum over topologies is related to the sum
over metrics with different regularity conditions. Here, we choose the
black hole-like regularity conditions, described by

\displaystyle b(0)=0\,\,,

\displaystyle r(0)=r_{+}\,\,,

\displaystyle\evaluated{(b'\alpha^{-1})}_{u=0}=1\,\,,

\displaystyle\evaluated{\alpha^{-1}(b'\alpha^{-1})'}_{u=0}=0\,\,,

\displaystyle\evaluated{\left(\frac{r'}{\alpha}\right)}_{u=0}=0\,\,,

(183)

where
r_{+}
is the horizon radius and a prime denotes the derivative
of a function in
u
, e.g.
b^{\prime}=\frac{db}{du}
.
The boundary conditions, as already stated, are such that the boundary
is described by a spherical shell, in this case with the boundary at

u=1
, with induced metric

\displaystyle ds_{\partial M}^{2}=b(1)^{2}d\tau^{2}+R^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,,

(184)

where
R
is the radius of the shell. As part of the boundary conditions,
we fix the radius of the shell
R
or equivalently its area
defined by

\displaystyle A=\Omega_{d-2}R^{d-2}\,\,,

(185)

where
\Omega_{d-2}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}
is the area of the unit

(d-2)
–sphere, with
\Gamma
being the gamma function.
For
d=4
, we have
\Omega_{4}=4\pi
and, for
d=5
,
we have
\Omega_{5}=2\pi^{2}
. We also fix the inverse temperature

\beta
at the boundary of space, which corresponds to the
component
b(1)
as

\displaystyle\beta=2\pi b(1)\,\,,

(186)

where
\beta=1/T
, with
T
being the temperature of the
heat reservoir.

We also need to provide regularity and
boundary conditions for the Maxwell field
A_{\alpha}

according to the regularity conditions of the metric
and to the ensemble in question. In spherical symmetry,
and without considering magnetic monopoles, the strength
field tensor is zero except for the component

F_{y\tau}=-F_{\tau y}=A_{\tau}^{\prime}
, where we choose a gauge
in which only the Maxwell component
A_{\tau}
is
non-zero. At
u=0
, we enforce the regularity condition

\displaystyle A(0)=0\,\,,

(187)

while at the boundary,
u=1
, we fix the electric potential
given by

\displaystyle\beta\phi=2\pi iA_{\tau}(1)\,\,.

(188)

We note that the correspondence between the electric potential
and the Maxwell field can be deduced by defining
\phi

as the electric potential measured by a stationary observer
in the physical Lorentzian spacetime and then use the Euclidean
Maxwell field instead of the physical one.

21.3
Action in spherical symmetry

Having the expression of the metric with the regularity and the
boundary conditions, we can now simplify the action in
Eq. (
179
) by using the explicit form of the
spherically symmetric metrics in Eq. (
182
).
By using the results of Chapter
3
, the
gravitational action can be written as

\displaystyle I_{g\mathrm{f}}=\left(\frac{2\pi br^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(1-\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u=1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\left(\frac{b^{\prime}r^{d-2}}{a}\right)\biggr|_{u=0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}abr^{d-2}G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x\,\,,

(189)

where

\displaystyle\mu=\frac{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}{(d-2)\Omega_{d-2}}\,\,,

(190)

and the Einstein tensor component
G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}
is given by

\displaystyle G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}=\frac{(d-2)}{2r^{\prime}r^{d-2}}\left(r^{d-3}\left(\frac{r^{\prime 2}}{a^{2}}-1\right)\right)^{\prime}\,\,.

(191)

Together with the regularity and boundary conditions in
Eqs. (
21.2
)–(
186
), the action in
Eq. (
21.3
) becomes

\displaystyle I_{g\mathrm{f}}=\left(\frac{\beta R^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(1-\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u=1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}r_{+}^{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}abr^{d-2}G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}d^{d}x\,\,.

(192)

The action for the Maxwell field can also be simplified using that

F^{\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}=2F_{u\tau}F^{u\tau}=2\frac{A_{\tau}^{\prime 2}}{b^{2}a^{2}}
.
It is more convenient to work with the integrand written as

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{g}}{4}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime 2}_{\tau}}{ba}+\left(\left(\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime}_{\tau}}{ba}\right)A_{\tau}\right)^{\prime}-\left(\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime}_{\tau}}{ba}\right)^{\prime}A_{\tau}\,\,,

(193)

and so the Maxwell action is given by

\displaystyle I_{A}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime 2}_{\tau}}{ba}d^{d}x-i\beta\phi\Omega_{d-2}\left(\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime}_{\tau}}{ba}\right)\biggr|_{u=1}-\int_{M}\left(\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime}_{\tau}}{ba}\right)^{\prime}A_{\tau}d^{d}x\,\,,

(194)

where the regularity and boundary conditions in Eqs. (
187
)
and (
188
) have been used. Finally,
putting together both actions, we have that the full action
I
is

\displaystyle I=\left(\frac{\beta R^{d-3}}{\mu}\left(1-\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)\right)\biggr|_{u=1}-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}r_{+}^{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}-i\beta\phi\Omega_{d-2}\left(\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime}_{\tau}}{ba}\right)\biggr|_{u=1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}\int_{M}abr^{d-2}\left(G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}-4\pi l_{p}^{d-2}\frac{A^{\prime 2}_{\tau}}{b^{2}a^{2}}\right)d^{d}x-\int_{M}\left(\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime}_{\tau}}{ba}\right)^{\prime}A_{\tau}d^{d}x\,\,,

(195)

which must be inserted in the Euclidean path integral

\displaystyle Z=\int DbDaDrDA_{\tau}\mathrm{e}^{-I}\,\,.

(196)

22
Zero loop approximation and the black hole solutions

22.1
The constrained path integral and reduced action

As a step towards the zero loop approximation, we constrain the path integral
along metrics that obey the constraint equations
that partially minimize the action. The constraint equations are composed
by the Hamiltonian constraint, momentum constraint and the Gauss constraint.
The momentum constraint is satisfied apriori since the metric is static.
The Hamiltonian
constraint is given by
G^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}=8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}T^{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\tau}}}
,
where
T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}=F_{\alpha\mu}F_{\beta\nu}g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\alpha\beta}F_{\nu\mu}F^{\nu\mu}

is the stress energy tensor of the Maxwell field. This Einstein equation is precisely
obtained by calculating the first order variation of the Euclidean action in the
metric component
b(u)
. In terms of the components of the
metric, the Hamiltonian constraint is given by

\displaystyle\frac{(d-2)}{2r^{\prime}r^{d-2}}\left(r^{d-3}\left(\frac{r^{\prime 2}}{a^{2}}-1\right)\right)^{\prime}=4\pi l_{p}^{d-2}\frac{A^{\prime 2}_{\tau}}{a^{2}b^{2}}\,\,.

(197)

The Gauss constraint
is given by the Maxwell equation
\nabla_{u}F^{\tau u}=0
,
which in terms of the metric and Maxwell field components yields

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{d-2}A^{\prime}_{\tau}}{ab}\right)^{\prime}=0\,\,,

(198)

which can be obtained by performing the first order variation
of the Euclidean action in the Maxwell component
A_{\tau}
.
The Gauss constraint can be first integrated to give

\displaystyle\frac{r^{d-2}}{ab}A^{\prime}_{\tau}=-i\frac{q}{\Omega_{d-2}}\,\,,

(199)

where
q
is the electric charge, having dimensions
of length
L^{\frac{d}{2}-2}
, where
L
is some unit of length.
Plugging this into the Hamiltonian
constraint, one can integrate Eq. (
197
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{2}=f(r;r_{+},q)=1-\frac{r_{+}^{d-3}}{r^{d-3}}-\frac{\lambda q^{2}}{r^{2d-6}}+\frac{\lambda q^{2}}{r^{2d-6}}\,,

(200)

where we used the regularity condition
r(0)=r_{+}
,
we defined
f(r;r_{+},q)
, and
\lambda
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda=\frac{8\pi l_{p}^{d-2}}{\Omega_{d-2}^{2}(d-2)(d-3)}\,\,.

(201)

The action in Eq. (
21.3
)
with the Hamiltonian and Gauss constraints imposed is called the reduced
action and assumes a simple expression, since the bulk terms disappear,
yielding

\displaystyle I_{*}[\beta,\phi,R;r_{+},q]=\frac{R^{d-3}\beta}{\mu}\left(1-\sqrt{f[R;r_{+},q]}\right)-q\beta\phi-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}r_{+}^{d-2}}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\,.

(202)

The reduced action
I_{*}
becomes a functional of the parameters

r_{+}
and
q
, and a function of the fixed parameters
\beta
,

\phi
and
R
. With the constraints applied to the path integral in
Eq. (
178
),
the integral over
b
can be neglected as the reduced action does not depend
on
b
. Also, the integral over
A_{\tau}
can also be neglected for the same reason.
The remaining integrals over
a
,
r
and
A_{\tau}
transform into
integrals over
r_{+}
and
q
. In order to see this, first one can perform
an arbitrary coordinate transformation
r=r(y)
, which gives a metric
only as functional of
b(y)
,
r_{+}
and
q
. And so, the sum over configurations
obeying the constraints must be done only in
r_{+}
and
q
. Then,
the constrained path integral
can be written as

\displaystyle Z[\beta,\phi,R]=\int D[r_{+}]D[q]\mathrm{e}^{-I_{*}[\beta,\phi,R;r_{+},q]}\,.

(203)

To proceed with the zero loop approximation, one must impose the
remaining Einstein-Maxwell equations. These equations are equivalent to
the conditions for the stationary points
of the reduced action in the plane
r_{+}\times q
.
For the zero loop approximation to remain valid, the stationary points must be
local minima of the action. The motivation of imposing the
constraint equations to the path integral is that we are able from
Eq. (
203
) to verify the stability of the solutions
given by the stationary point, at least along the hypersurface of
metrics obeying the constraints, see
[
115
]
.

22.2
Stationary points of the reduced action

The partition function in Eq. (
203
) describes the
grand canonical ensemble of a charged black hole inside a cavity constrained
to the hypersurface where the Hamiltonian and Gauss constraints
are satisfied. Here, we are interested in performing the full zero loop
approximation of the path integral to obtain the equilibrium
solutions for the black hole. The solutions are described by the
stationary points of the reduced action in Eq. (
202
),
which satisfy the conditions

\displaystyle\frac{\partial I_{*}}{\partial r_{+}}=0\,,

(204)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial I_{*}}{\partial q}=0\,.

(205)

These two conditions can be written in terms of the fixed variables of the
ensemble,
\beta
and
\phi
, and the variables evaluated at the
stationary points,
r_{+}
and
q
, as

\displaystyle\beta=\frac{4\pi}{(d-3)}\frac{r_{+}^{2d-5}}{r_{+}^{2d-6}-\lambda q^{2}}\sqrt{f[R,r_{+},q]}\,,

(206)

\displaystyle\phi=\frac{q}{(d-3)\Omega_{d-2}\sqrt{f[R,r_{+},q]}}\left(\frac{1}{r_{+}^{d-3}}-\frac{1}{R^{d-3}}\right)\,,

(207)

respectively. In order to find the solutions of the
ensemble, one must solve the inverse problem of
the system in Eqs. (
206
)
and (
207
) to have the
functions
r_{+}=r_{+}(\beta,\phi,R)
and
q=q(\beta,\phi,R)
.
The reduced action evaluated at the stationary points

r_{+}=r_{+}(\beta,\phi,R)
and
q=q(\beta,\phi,R)
is defined
as

\displaystyle I_{0}[\beta,\phi,R]=I_{*}[\beta,\phi,R;r_{+}[\beta,\phi,R],q[\beta,\phi,R]]\,.

(208)

Using the expression of the reduced action in
Eq. (
202
),
the action
I_{0}
can be further written as

\displaystyle I_{0}[\beta,\phi,R]=

\displaystyle\frac{R^{d-3}\beta}{\mu}\left(1-\sqrt{f[R;r_{+}[\beta,\phi,R],q[\beta,\phi,R]]}\right)

\displaystyle-q[\beta,\phi,R]\beta\phi-\frac{\Omega_{d-2}r_{+}^{d-2}[\beta,\phi,R]}{4l_{p}^{d-2}}\,.

(209)

In the zero loop approximation, the partition function
is given solely by the contribution of the stationary point,
i.e.

\displaystyle Z[\beta,\phi,R]=\mathrm{e}^{-I_{0}[\beta,\phi,R]}\,,

(210)

where
I_{0}[\beta,\phi,R]
is taken from
Eq. (
209
).

We analyze now the solutions of the stationary conditions.
It is useful to make the following definitions

\displaystyle\gamma=\frac{16\pi^{2}R^{2}}{(d-3)^{2}}\frac{\Phi^{2}}{\beta^{2}(1-\Phi^{2})^{2}}\,,

(211)

\displaystyle\Phi=(d-3)\Omega_{d-2}\sqrt{\lambda}\phi\,,

(212)

\displaystyle x=\frac{r_{+}}{R}\,,

(213)

\displaystyle y=\frac{\lambda q^{2}}{R^{2d-6}}\,.

(214)

The parameter
\gamma
takes the role of
the temperature
T=\frac{1}{\beta}
, while

\Phi
takes the role of
\phi
. The radius
of the reservoir
R
is taken to be a scale,
that can be absorbed in the variables
r_{+}

and
q
, having thus the horizon radius in units
of
R
, yielding
x
, and the charge squared in units of
R
multiplied
by the ratio between the Planck length and
R
, yielding
y
.
The system of equations in Eqs. (
206
) and (
207
)
can be inverted to give equations for
x
and
y
.
Inverting Eq. (
207
),
i.e.
y=\frac{x^{2d-6}\Phi^{2}}{1-(1-\Phi^{2})x^{d-3}}
, and
substituting into
Eq. (
206
), one arrives to the
equation

\displaystyle(1-\Phi^{2})x^{d-1}-x^{2}+\frac{\Phi^{2}}{\gamma}=0\,.

(215)

Now using Eq. (
215
) into

y=\frac{x^{2d-6}\Phi^{2}}{1-(1-\Phi^{2})x^{d-3}}
, one gets
the second equation as

\displaystyle y=\gamma x^{2(d-2)}\,.

(216)

Hence, the solutions to the stationary conditions
are obtained by solving Eq. (
215
)
for
x(\gamma,\Phi)
and the value of
y(\gamma,\Phi)
can then be
read off from Eq. (
216
).

The solutions for the horizon radius satisfying the polynomial
equation Eq. (
215
) can only be found analytically
for specific values of
d
. For example, analytical solutions can be found
for
d=4
as we have a third order polynomial equation for
x

[
130
]
, and
also for
d=5
as we have a second order polynomial equation for
x^{2}
. We analyze this last case
below, separately. For generic
d
, however, it is not possible
to find an analytic expression for
x
.

Notwithstanding, we can study the qualitative behaviour of Eq. (
215
)
in terms of the dimension
d
and the parameters
\gamma
, which represents the fixed temperature
of the ensemble, and
\Phi
, which represents the fixed electric potential of the
ensemble. We impose that the solutions for
x
and
y
must be physical. This is so
since the Riemannian space that minimizes the action must be correspondent to
a Lorentzian space through the inverse Wick rotation. Here, we assume that
the black hole must lie inside the cavity and that it must be subextremal, i.e.

\displaystyle 0\leq x<1\,,

(217)

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{y}{x^{2(d-3)}}<1\,,

(218)

respectively. One can use Eq. (
216
)
to rewrite the last condition Eq. (
218
) into

\displaystyle\gamma x^{2}<1\,\,.

(219)

Moreover, using Eq. (
215
) in
Eq. (
219
), one obtains a condition
for
\Phi
as

\displaystyle 0\leq\Phi^{2}<1\,.

(220)

Therefore, the conditions for physical solutions reduce to the
restrictions of the parameters
\gamma
and
\Phi
through
Eqs. (
219
) and (
220
), respectively.

In order to study further Eq. (
215
), it is useful
to define

\displaystyle h(x)=(1-\Phi^{2})x^{d-1}-x^{2}+\frac{\Phi^{2}}{\gamma}\,.

(221)

The values of the function
h(x)
at the boundaries established
by the condition Eq. (
217
) are

h(0)=frac{\Phi^{2}}{\gamma}>0
and
h(1)=h(0)(1-\gamma)
.
The parameter
\gamma
, although being restricted by Eq. (
219
),
can assume values higher than unity. Since
\gamma
is proportional to
the temperature,
\gamma
can attain large values for large values of the
temperature, for fixed
\phi
or
\Phi
. We thus need to separate the
analysis into three regions,
\gamma<1
,
\gamma=1
, and
\gamma>1
.

Starting with
\gamma<1
, one has that
h(x)
is positive at the
boundaries
h(0)>0
and
h(1)>0
. To further gain knowledge on the amount of zeros,
one must compute the zeros of the first derivative
h^{\prime}(x)
and
the sign of the second derivative
h^{\prime\prime}(x)
. The derivatives
are given by

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(x)=x(d-1)(1-\Phi^{2})\left(x^{d-3}-\frac{2}{(d-1)(1-\Phi^{2})}\right)\,\,,

(222)

\displaystyle h^{\prime\prime}(x)=(d-1)(d-2)(1-\Phi^{2})x^{d-3}-2\,\,.

(223)

The derivative of
h(x)
vanishes at a bifurcation point

\displaystyle x_{\mathrm{bif}}=\left(\frac{2}{(d-1)(1-\Phi^{2})}\right)^{\frac{1}{d-3}}\,,

(224)

i.e.
h^{\prime}(x_{\mathrm{bif}})=0
,
and the second derivative is positive there, i.e.
h^{\prime\prime}(x_{\mathrm{bif}})>0
.
So, the bifurcation point
x_{\mathrm{bif}}
marks the location of the only minimum
of
h(x)
. In the case of
\gamma<1
, if the location of the minimum
lies out of bounds,
x_{\mathrm{bif}}>1
, then it is certain that
there are no zeros of
h(x)
in the interval
0\leq x<1
. If the minimum
lies in the interval
0<x_{\mathrm{bif}}<1
, then
h(x)
may have one or two
zeros in
0\leq x<1
. This happens for the range of

0\leq\Phi^{2}<\frac{d-3}{d-1}
. However, in order for the zeros to exist,
one must require that
h(x_{\mathrm{bif}})<0
, which only happens when

\displaystyle\gamma\geq\gamma_{\mathrm{bif}}(\Phi,d)\equiv\frac{(d-1)^{\frac{d-1}{d-3}}}{4^{\frac{1}{d-3}}(d-3)}\Phi^{2}(1-\Phi^{2})^{\frac{2}{d-3}}\,.

(225)

Summarizing the results in the interval
\gamma<1
and

0\leq\Phi^{2}<\frac{d-3}{d-1}
, for

\displaystyle\gamma<\gamma_{\mathrm{bif}}\,,

(226)

there are no solutions. For

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathrm{bif}}\leq\gamma<1\,,

(227)

there are two solutions. The solutions
can be denominated by
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
,
with
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
. Moreover, one must
have

\displaystyle x_{1}\leq x_{\mathrm{bif}}\leq x_{2}\,.

(228)

When the equality is reached in Eq. (
227
),
i.e.
\gamma=\gamma_{\mathrm{bif}}
, the two solutions
merge into
x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{\mathrm{bif}}
, hence the nomenclature of

x_{\mathrm{bif}}
as the bifurcation point. For

\gamma<1
and
\frac{d-3}{d-1}\leq\Phi^{2}<1
,
there are no solutions.

For
\gamma=1
, the function
h(x)
has always one zero at
x=1
.
We note that this point is a critical point since the
derivatives of the action are not well-defined there. If

0\leq\Phi^{2}<\frac{d-3}{d-1}
, the other zero lies in the
interval
0<x<1
and so it corresponds to
x_{1}
while
x_{2}=1
.
For the case of equality
\Phi^{2}=\frac{d-3}{d-1}
, one has

x_{1}=x_{2}=1
. For
\frac{d-3}{d-1}<\Phi^{2}<1
,
x_{1}=1

while
x_{2}
lies out of bounds as
x_{2}>1
and it is unphysical.

Figure 11
:

Left plot: Stationary points of the action are plotted, with
x_{1}
in blue
and
x_{2}
in red, in function of
RT
, for a constant
\phi=0.02
and
for four values of
d
:
d=4
in dotted lines,
d=5
in dashed lines,
d=7
in
solid lines, and
d=10
in dot dashed lines.
Right plot: Stationary points of the action are plotted, with
x_{1}
in blue and

x_{2}
in red, in function of
\phi
, for a constant
RT=0.3
, and the maximum
value of
\phi
(in orange) corresponding to
\Phi=1
, for four values
of
d
:
d=4
in dotted lines,
d=5
in dashed lines,
d=7
in
solid lines, and
d=10
in dot dashed lines. Here,
l_{p}
is set to one.

For
\gamma>1
, the function
h(x)
has always one zero,
x_{1}
,
in the interval
0\leq x<1
, while the remaining zero
x_{2}

lies in the interval
x>1
, all of this for
0\leq\Phi^{2}<1
.
The zero
x_{1}
is physical while the zero
x_{2}
is unphysical.

We must highlight some important comments on the behaviour of the solutions.
From the definition of
\gamma
in Eq. (
211
),
one has that
\gamma\propto\frac{R^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\propto(RT)^{2}
.
Considering the solution
x_{2}
in function of the temperature

RT
, one has that
x_{2}
exists with an image in the interval

0<x_{2}<1
for a finite range of
T=\frac{1}{\beta}
and
for
\Phi^{2}<\frac{d-3}{d-1}
. Specifically, for

RT=\frac{d-3}{4\pi\absolutevalue{\Phi}}(1-\Phi^{2})
, i.e.
\gamma=1
,
one has
x_{2}=1
and so for higher values of
RT
, the solution
x_{2}

goes out of bounds and becomes unphysical. This behaviour is not
present in the neutrally charged case, analyzed in
[
102
]
.
The plot of the solutions
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
as functions of
RT
for
a constant
\phi=0.02
and for four values of
d
is shown in
the left part of Fig.
11
, with
l_{p}=1
.
Also, the plot of the solutions
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
as functions of

\phi
for a constant
RT=0.3
and for four values of
d
is shown in
the right part of
Fig.
11
, with
l_{p}=1
. In these plots, we chose
the quantity
\phi
instead of
\Phi
since we want to showcase
the full dependence in the dimension
d
. Indeed,
\phi
is the fixed
independent parameter at the cavity while
\Phi
is a quantity
proportional to
d
.

22.3
Beyond zero loop approximation and
stability of the stationary points

With the stationary points analyzed, we proceed with the analysis
of their stability. In order to probe the stability, we
can go beyond the zero loop approximation of the reduced action.
The reduced action is to be expanded around the stationary points
up to second order terms, i.e. the reduced action
can be written as

\displaystyle I_{*}[\beta,\phi,R;r_{+},q]=I_{0}[\beta,\phi,R]+\sum_{ij}{I_{*0}}_{ij}\delta i\delta j\,,

(229)

where
I_{0}[\beta,\phi,R]

is defined generically in Eq. (
208
)
and specifically for the grand canonical ensemble
of a charged black hole in
Eq. (
209
),
and

{I_{*0}}_{ij}
are
the second derivatives of the reduced action

{I_{*}}_{ij}=\frac{\partial^{2}I_{*}}{\partial i\partial j}
evaluated
at an extremum of the action, with

i
and
j
being either
r_{+}
or
q
. The partition function
with this expansion around a particular stationary point is given
by

\displaystyle Z[\beta,\phi,R]={\mathrm{e}}^{-I_{0}[\beta,\phi,R]}\int D[\delta q]D[\delta r_{+}]{\mathrm{e}}^{-\sum_{ij}{I_{*0}}_{ij}\delta i\delta j}\,.

(230)

In order to have a well-defined path integral in this approximation,
which takes into account the static one loop corrections obeying the
Hamiltonian and Gauss constraints, the exponent must be always negative,
i.e. the stationary point must be a minimum of the reduced action.

To obtain the condition of stability in terms of the solutions
of the ensemble, one must analyze the hessian of the reduced
action, which has in this case the components

\displaystyle{I_{*0}}_{r_{+}r_{+}}=\frac{(d-2)\Omega_{d-2}R^{d-3}\beta}{16\pi\sqrt{f}r_{+}^{2}l_{p}^{d-2}}\mathcal{I}_{r_{+}r_{+}}\,,

(231)

\displaystyle{I_{*0}}_{r_{+}q}=\frac{(d-2)\Omega_{d-2}R^{d-3}\beta}{16\pi\sqrt{f}r_{+}ql_{p}^{d-2}}\mathcal{I}_{r_{+}q}\,,

(232)

\displaystyle{I_{*0}}_{qq}=\frac{(d-2)\Omega_{d-2}R^{d-3}\beta}{16\pi\sqrt{f}q^{2}l_{p}^{2}}\mathcal{I}_{qq}\,,

(233)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{r_{+}r_{+}}{\hskip-5.69054pt}=\frac{d-3}{fx^{2d-6}}\Big[\frac{d-3}{2}\left(x^{2d-6}-y\right)^{2}{\hskip-5.69054pt}-\left(x^{2d-6}-(2d-5)y\right)\left(1-x^{d-3}\right)\left(x^{d-3}{\hskip-5.69054pt}-y\right)\Big]\,,

(234)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{r_{+}q}=-\frac{(d-3)}{x^{d-3}}\frac{\left(2x^{d-3}-x^{2d-6}-y\right)}{x^{d-3}-y}y\,,

(235)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{qq}=2\frac{1-x^{d-3}}{x^{d-3}-y}y\,.

(236)

For the stationary point to be a minimum, the matrix
{I_{*0}}_{ij}

must be positive definite. In turn, this condition can be translated
into the principal minors being positive through the
Sylvester criterion, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{r_{+}r_{+}}>0\,,

(237)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{r_{+}r_{+}}\mathcal{I}_{qq}-\mathcal{I}_{r_{+}q}^{2}>0\,.

(238)

In this case,
\mathcal{I}_{qq}
is always positive and so
the last condition, Eq. (
238
), is
sufficient to ensure positive definiteness.
Using Eqs. (
234
)-(
236
)
and (
216
), the condition in
Eq. (
238
) reduces to
